- **host_summary:** Claude's PostCompact `compact_summary`, labelled as written by the host.
- **event:** session_start, compact, session_end, off_record, on_record, model_switch, interrupt, stop_failure, host_prompt, gap.

Prompts you didn't type are stored as events, never as your words. On Claude these are scheduled tasks, /loop runs, background subagent reports and messages from other sessions. Codex prompts that carry `agent_id` are also stored as events. So are the prompts of a Claude chat a program runs, told by `CLAUDE_CODE_ENTRYPOINT` being `sdk-cli` (`claude -p`), `sdk-ts` or `sdk-py`; its replies and tool lines are kept, a record control in its prompts is still honoured, and transcript recovery never stores its prompts either. Any other value, such as `cli`, `claude-desktop`, `claude-desktop-3p`, `local-agent` or `claude-vscode`, or none, counts as a person's chat. Each heartbeat records the cleaned entrypoint (32 characters) and whether it was a program's chat. Codex `codex exec` runs are not told apart: nothing known marks them. Built-in slash commands are not stored.

A chat a program runs starts no update of its own: its SessionEnd, PreCompact, Stop backlog and prompt triggers queue nothing, and the chat-start catch-up never takes its own file, though it still takes the project's other idle sessions. The update job passes over a session whose inbox file holds no prompt the user typed and whose catch-up record marks it a program's chat, or that, once its window reaches the end of the file, holds a host prompt: it records `skipped (headless)` and moves the watermark past the window with no model call, to the end of the file unless a marked chat's backlog runs on past the window (see "A program's chat" under "Job control"). A person's chat that holds host prompts, from scheduled tasks, /loop runs or subagent reports, or a typed prompt anywhere, is updated as before. Codex `codex exec` runs can't be told apart and stay as they are.

An entry is referred to as `s:<sid8>#<line>`, and `eva-md show` resolves those refs. It also accepts the full session id in place of the first 8 characters, which settles a ref that two sessions share. Because entries are JSON-escaped, pasted text can never forge a speaker or an entry boundary.

**Redaction.** Applied in the hook before any byte reaches disk, again on model output, and on the staged diff before each commit. Rules:
- private key blocks
- AWS AKIA, ASIA, ABIA and ACCA ids and secret keys
- GitHub `gh*_` and `github_pat_` tokens; GitLab `glpat-`, `gldt-`, `glrt-`, `glptt-` and `gloas-` tokens
- Slack `xox*-` and `xapp-` tokens and webhook URLs, Discord webhook URLs and Telegram bot tokens in a Bot API address
- OpenAI `sk-`, `sk-proj-`, `sk-svcacct-` and `sk-admin-`, Anthropic `sk-ant-`, Google `AIza`, and Stripe `sk_` and `rk_` keys; 1Password `ops_` service tokens
- Hugging Face `hf_` and npm `npm_` tokens that mix capitals, small letters and digits; PyPI `pypi-AgE…`, HashiCorp Vault `hvs.` and `hvb.`, DigitalOcean `dop_v1_`, `doo_v1_` and `dor_v1_`, SendGrid `SG.`, Shopify `shpat_`, `shpca_`, `shppa_` and `shpss_`, and Docker `dckr_pat_` tokens
- JWTs; Authorization Bearer and Basic values; `user:password@` inside URLs
- the value in `key=value` or `key: value` when the key looks like password, secret, token, api_key, access_key or client_secret and the value is 8 characters or more
- the same for a key whose last part is `PASS` or `PWD` after one or more separators or a change of case, such as `DB_PASS`, `MYSQL_PWD`, `SMTP__PASS`, `Db__Pwd` or `userPwd`, with a quoted value taken whole, spaces too, unless it starts or ends with a blank, as code between two strings does; and for `pass` or `pwd` alone after `?`, `&` or `;`, as in a URL query or a connection string's `Pwd=`, or as `--pass=`. A key ending in `pass` or `Pass`, as in `render_pass`, `onPass` or `Smtp__Pass`, and `pass` or `pwd` alone anywhere else, as in YAML or an object literal, lose a value only when it looks like a password (below). A path after a `pwd` key is kept, since `PWD` and its kin hold a folder, and so is `PASS:` in capitals, a test result
- a MySQL or MariaDB client's `-p<password>` (`mysql`, `mysqldump`, `mariadb-dump`, `mariabackup` and the like), the password in curl's `-u`, `--user`, `-U` or `--proxy-user` `user:password` (a `-u` or `-U` alone or after short options that take no value, as in `-sSu`), the one sshpass takes after `-p` among its own options, the one `docker login` or `podman login` takes after `-p` or `--password` (never `--password-stdin`, nor another command's `-p`, a port), the one `redis-cli` takes after `-a` or `--pass`, and openssl's `-pass`, `-passin` or `-passout` `pass:<password>` (never its `env:`, `file:`, `fd:` or `stdin` forms), when the command's name starts a command word. Every such option of the command is read, and only the command's own: it ends at a line end, an unquoted `;`, `|`, `&&`, `||`, a `&` that sends it to the background (never a redirection's, as in `2>&1`, nor one between two characters of a word, as in an unquoted URL query's `?a=1&b=2`, nor any of these inside a bracket the command opened, as in `$(cd x && cat y)`), a backtick or a `)` it didn't open; a command written over several lines with backslashes is read across them, and a quoted string is one word, whatever it holds, though a command inside it is read on its own, within the quotes, as in `sh -c 'curl -u …'`, up to that string's closing quote when the name follows the quote. A command is read twice. The first reading pairs each quote with the next of its kind on the line, so a `;` in a quoted header or SQL statement ends nothing, and `\'` inside bash's `$'…'` is escaped; the second pairs a quote only when its string is a word of its own, and also takes a backtick substitution that is a word of its own, as in ``mysql -h `cat host` -p…``, as part of the command, where the first ends at the backtick as inline code in Markdown does. Whatever either reading takes is replaced, so a quote the first pairs wrongly, as the apostrophe in `o'brien` or a quote inside `"$(…)"`, can't hide a password, and the second reads an option's value as the first does, so both take a password the same way. An option where another option's value was due, as the second `-u` of `-u -u admin:…` or the `-p` of `sshpass -d -p …`, is read as the option. An sshpass that a later word of another starts, as in `sshpass -e ssh jump sshpass -p …` or `$(sshpass -p …)`, has its own options read, which end as the first's do or at a `)` that closes a bracket opened before it. An option's value is attached to it or the next word after blanks, quoted in whole or in part, as in `admin:'S3cret pw'`, and a `:` inside the marker of a token that names curl's user is not the one before its password. A variable, a command substitution or a placeholder there is kept, except inside single quotes, where the shell expands nothing
- a password in prose, after password, passwd, pass or pw and is or was ("my password is Hunter2!x"), when the word looks like a password: 6 characters or more with letters mixed with digits or symbols, or 12 or more letters that mix cases at random. An ordinary word, words joined by `-`, `_`, `.`, `/` or `'`, digits alone, a technical term such as `base64`, `sha256` or `utf-8`, a measurement on its own, such as `16.6ms` or `1.25GB`, where a whole number takes a unit of two letters or more, as in `120ms`, a path, a placeholder, code and a variable are kept
- high-entropy strings next to such words

Matches become `[REDACTED:<kind>]`. Git SHAs and UUIDs are kept in ordinary text, but not as the value of a secret-looking key, a URL password or an Authorization header, where they may be the secret. `op://` references are kept. If redaction fails, nothing is stored (fail closed).

A `key=value` or `key: value` on a line of its own (dotenv, YAML, INI, properties) takes the value to the end of the line, across `& ; , ( ) < > { } [ ]`, while code shapes such as calls and `$VAR` references are kept. The text removed is the union of what the baseline rules (`internal/redact/baseline`) and the current rules remove, judged on the same cleaned text, so a rule change can only remove more. Identity fields get only the format rules, not the entropy rule, so names like `token-refresh-worker` stay intact.

**Retention.** No time limit and no automatic deletion. Uninstall keeps the inbox; `uninstall --apply --all` removes it with the rest of the local data (see "Install and uninstall"). The doctor reports its size, roughly 0.1 to 0.3 GB a year.

**Removal.** `eva-md inbox clear` removes captured sessions whole, never a line of one, since the JSONL is never rewritten: those `--session` names (repeatable; a full id, or the first 8 characters a ref keeps, or a ref `s:<id>#<line>`), those of the project `--project` names by its key, its inbox folder's name, and those last written before the local day `--before` names, by the file's modification time (the watermark's, for a file already gone); given together, all must match. A filter given an empty value, as a script's unset variable gives it, is a usage error, never taken for one not given, which would widen the removal, and a day `--before` is given always filters, 0001-01-01 too, which in UTC is the zero time: it chooses nothing. 8 characters two sessions share, with neither's whole id given, choose nothing, and the error names both. A session whose file is gone while its watermark stays, as a file deleted by hand leaves it, counts too. Without `--apply` it previews: each session with its host, id, project, last write and size (20, then a count), how many refs in the wiki's thread pages name them, and that the wiki and its history keep what updates wrote; it changes nothing. `--apply` takes the update's lock without waiting, so no update writes a watermark again for a file removed, and the search index lock of each project concerned, waiting up to 5 s for a search's top-up; while either is held it removes nothing and says to try again. It then takes each session under its session lock, waiting up to 10 s for a hook, and stopping there, saying to run it again, while one still holds it: its transcript recovery record goes when it would append to a project folder whose capture goes, or can't be read, so a cursor behind the hooks can never bring removed rows back, but a blocked one, as a chat off the record has, stays: it may be all that keeps the chat off the record, when the markers couldn't be written, and it brings nothing back, since a blocked record recovers no row and leaving the block starts again at the transcript's end; then it appends a line for the session to `inbox/.removed` (JSON: v, at, host, session; fsynced, under a flock), just before its files go, whose removal Ctrl-C no longer stops, so a removal that stops at a session a hook holds, on Ctrl-C or on a failure records no session it keeps, short of a file it can't remove or a kill in between; then each session file, then its watermark, so a removal cut short leaves at most a watermark, which the next removal takes. Last it deletes the search index folder of each project it removed from, so the removed words leave the disk at once rather than at the catch-ups after (see "Search index"); `eva-md index` or the daily maintain builds it again, and search scans those files meanwhile. A project folder left empty goes too. The session markers stay, since they hold no chat text and `off_record` and `was_off` keep a chat still open off the record, and so does the session's empty lock file, which a hook may already hold open. A chat still open starts a new file at its next turn. The wiki, its history and the hosts' own transcripts are never touched. The daily lint reads `inbox/.removed` (see "Health check"), and so do `eva-md show` and `eva_show` when a ref finds no line, to say that its chat was removed with `eva-md inbox clear` rather than that the source wasn't found. `inbox list` and `inbox show` are planned; `eva-md search --all` shows each inbox hit's project key and ref.

---

## Flows

### Capture (every hook event)

1. The host runs the plugin hook, which calls the binary with the payload on stdin. On Codex the command ends with `|| echo '{}'`, so Stop always gets valid output even if the binary is missing.
2. **Guards.** Exit at once (printing `{}` on Codex Stop, nothing elsewhere) in any of these cases:
   - `EVA_MD_INNER=1`
   - a config that exists but can't be read, which a chat start says in one line (step 6 of "Resume: new chat or /clear")
   - `CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE=true`
   - the path or remote is excluded
   - the path is the wiki, a state folder or a temp folder
   - a Codex memory-writing run (`cwd` ending in `.codex/memories`, or a prompt starting with `## Memory Writing Agent`), so Codex's own background memory jobs are never stored as your chats (pattern from ai-memory; not yet observed live, see Open host questions)

   In a desktop spare folder (`~/.claude/spares`), only the model marker is stored. In a chat that is off the record, only markers are stored. A run that stops at the folder, remote, wiki, state, temp or memory-run guard, or in a spare folder, writes no heartbeat but stamps `heartbeats/<host>/uncaptured.json` with its time alone, never the folder, session or event, rewritten in place without fsync: the host still keeps that chat, and the capture-freshness check reads the stamp's first record as a hook of the host's that ran (see "Health check"). The time is written in UTC at one width, over the last stamp and never truncating it, with no lock, so runs that stamp at once, as the async hooks of parallel tool calls do, leave one whole stamp. The hook-failure count never reads it. The `EVA_MD_INNER` and `CLAUDE_CODE_REMOTE` guards stamp nothing.
3. Resolve the project and branch from the payload's `cwd`, not `CLAUDE_PROJECT_DIR` (which stays at the start folder when Claude enters a worktree). Cached per worktree (see Identity resolver). Then a catch-up pass (at most 100 ms) reads this session's known transcript up to its end. Until it has, the event keeps only its fixed event name and no brief, because unread rows may hold an off-the-record control a hook missed. A record control in a prompt you typed is honoured before this pass, so it never waits on it.
4. **UserPromptSubmit.**
   - Check for "off the record" and "back on the record".
   - If the prompt came from a scheduled task, /loop, a subagent, another session or a program-run Claude chat, store it as a `host_prompt` event. Otherwise redact it and append a prompt entry.
   - If this session has not been briefed, return the brief (the first-prompt fallback). If it has, return the hash-triggered refresh when the thread's Now or the standing rules changed.
   - Add pointers to the system pages the prompt names, the knowledge the prompt strongly matches and, on the first prompt and then every `reminder_every` prompts, the memory reminder (see "Per prompt" under Recall and fencing). None go out in a program-run chat, off the record, for a prompt you didn't type or a built-in command, or with a wiki of another instance.
   - If the session's inbox file last changed 30 minutes or more before this prompt, with 600 bytes or more unprocessed, spawn an update for the session: the burst before the gap counts as a finished chat. The file's modification time and size come from one stat taken before anything is appended, transcript recovery included; its watermark is read only when both thresholds hold. Host prompts, built-in commands and the record controls spawn nothing.
5. **PostToolUse / PostToolUseFailure** (async): append one tool or plan line and print nothing. The output of an async hook would reach the model a turn later.
6. **Stop.**
   - Append the reply. A missing or null reply is stored as a `gap: missing final reply` event; the catch-up pass recovers the reply from the transcript when it can.
   - If 60 KB or more of the session's inbox file is unprocessed once the reply is stored (its size minus the watermark: one stat, and the watermark is read only once the file is that large), spawn an update for the session. This is the trigger for desktop chats that never end.
   - On Claude print nothing: additional context there would continue the conversation. On Codex print `{}`.
7. **StopFailure** (Claude, API errors): append a `stop_failure` event with the error type, so the brief says "no reply (stop_failure: rate_limit)" instead of guessing.
8. **PreCompact.** Append a compact event after the catch-up pass, and spawn an update for the session. Never block, and on Codex never print `continue:false`.
9. **Other events.**
   - PostCompact (Claude): store `compact_summary` as a host_summary entry, unless the chat has been off the record since its last startup or clear (`was_off`, see "Off the record"): then only the compact event.
   - PostModelSwitch (Claude): record `to_model` on the session, including `source=resume`, and print nothing.
   - Interrupt (Codex): append an interrupt marker.
10. **SessionEnd.** Append a session_end event and spawn an update for the session, within 200 ms (a p95 of about 45 ms in the benchmark, the spawn included). Claude's plugin SessionEnd budget is a fixed 1.5 s. Codex's is 1 s by default and 3 s at most.
11. Every event updates its heartbeat and exits 0. A spawn the event asked for starts after its output is written (see "Background update"), so it never delays or holds the output. The first event of the local day also spawns maintain (see "Daily commit and push"). Each event's heartbeat keeps its last 20 runs, each marked failed on a panic, an identity failure, a failed session lock, marker read or write or inbox append, or a capture that ran out of time, which is recorded after the output, within 100 ms. Each run also records its time (`duration_ms`), from the hook's start to just before its heartbeat is saved, so it leaves out the process's start and the save itself; a run out of time records its time once its output is written. The heartbeat also says when the run's transcript lies outside `~/.claude/projects` or `~/.codex/sessions`, the folders transcript recovery reads, as it does once `CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR` or `CODEX_HOME` moves the host's folder (`transcript_elsewhere`); recovery reads none of that chat's transcript. The heartbeat judges by recovery's own rule (`catchup.InStore`): the folder and the transcript's own folder count where their symlinks lead, so a `~/.claude` or `~/.codex` linked elsewhere, as a dotfiles setup links it, is still the host's folder, and a transcript path a host writes through a link to the home lies in it: recovery reads such a transcript, opening it by the path its folder leads to, never through a link; a link from inside the folder to elsewhere leads out of it, and a folder not made yet is judged by its path as written. A run out of time judges it once its output is written. A home that is itself a symlink isn't supported (see "Shared"). Transcript recovery also records drift: three Stops in a row whose reply was read over only rows it doesn't recognize write `catchup/drift-<host>.json`, rewritten on each such Stop and keeping its first time, until a recognized row clears it; a new transcript baseline restarts the count. A chat that is off the record, or whose transcript recovery is behind, spawns no update, and neither does any chat with `updates = false` or a config without a wiki. Nor does a Claude chat a program runs (`CLAUDE_CODE_ENTRYPOINT` `sdk-cli`, `sdk-ts` or `sdk-py`): its SessionEnd, PreCompact, Stop and prompt triggers ask for no update of its own, and its chat start leaves its own file out of the catch-up but still takes the project's other idle sessions (see "Triggers"). The day's maintain starts all the same whenever the config names a wiki path and an instance, or no wiki at all, for its search index step.

**Host differences:**
- **Claude.** The model arrives only at SessionStart (optional, often missing after /clear) and PostModelSwitch. Stop does not fire after an Esc interrupt, so catch-up covers it.
- **Codex.** `model` arrives on every event except SessionEnd. `turn_id` arrives only on turn events. `transcript_path` may be null. The session then keeps the transcript it registered before, under the same rules as an event that names it: a change of project, folder or branch, or the chat going off the record, starts again at the transcript's end, and a transcript that can no longer be opened marks the session missing (`transcript_unavailable`). Only a session that never named a transcript gets no recovery; this is counted and reported. Codex hooks run only after you trust them in /hooks.

### Resume: new chat or /clear

1. SessionStart(startup|clear) passes the guards, runs catch-up for this project's idle sessions (at most 150 ms; it looks at the 256 most recently updated of at most 4,096 recovery records per host, and the daily maintain keeps at most 2,048 a host, so it lists them all unless more than 2,048 chats of a host start between two of its runs) and appends a session_start event with its source and model. In a spare folder, store only the model marker and exit; with no briefed marker, the first prompt from a real folder gets the brief.
2. **Pick the previous chat.** This is the newest session in this project and branch, on either host, that did real work (more than 2 exchanges, or any tool or plan activity), excluding this session. Chats are ordered by when they last did work: the newest prompt, reply, tool or plan entry in the last MiB, or the newest entry of any kind only when the last MiB holds none; ties go to the newest entry, then host and session id. A session_end, session_start, compact or interrupt event never makes an idle chat the previous chat, the lineage pick or a new thread's parent. The brief reads at most the 64 most recently changed session files of the project, counted as files whatever number of chats it keeps from them, and those 64 are the files it lists for the start-of-chat catch-up (step 7). A file it finds to hold a chat a program ran (see below) doesn't count toward the 64, so a burst of them can't push your chats out of the brief; up to 64 such files, and 2 MiB of them in all, are read and listed beyond the 64, and past either bound they count as any file does. Each holds its first line in the last MiB the brief reads, so none is longer than that, and a MiB of the short tool lines and replies such a chat holds takes about 13 ms to read, so they add about 25 ms at most. It decodes the last MiB of each and only counts the lines before it, and it scans those earlier bytes for work only when a branch's last MiB shows none, so its cost stays bounded however long the chats grow. That scan reads line by line, and work there (a tool or plan entry, or more than 2 prompts and 2 replies) counts only for the branch it was done on, read from the line's branch field (none means `default`); it stops once every branch that needs it has shown work, and reads whole the line the last MiB cuts in two, so its branch is known. A chat a program ran is never the previous chat, the lineage pick below, or one of the earlier chats or other branches the brief lists: one whose session file, read from its first line, holds host prompts and no prompt the user typed. The whole file is judged, every branch, as the update job judges it, so a program's chat that checks out a branch is passed over on that branch too, and a person's chat that checked out another branch is a person's on every branch. A program's chat longer than the MiB the brief reads is not told apart. The lineage that names a new thread page's parent follows the same rule.
   - If there is none, and this is a startup or clear on a branch with no Now page of its own, use the lineage fallback: the project's most recently active thread, labelled "continuing from <branch>", and its Now labelled with that branch. Resumed and forked chats never use lineage.
   - The general area only counts a chat whose newest work (prompt, reply, tool or plan) is less than 12 hours old, with the one-minute future guard on that time too, so a fresh event such as a session_end doesn't keep a day-old general chat in the window. A session file last changed more than 12 hours ago is not read at all, since every entry is written before its file last changes; it is still listed for the start-of-chat catch-up.
3. Load the thread's Now section, reading only that page (and the project's now.md to confirm the slug) by its path, never the rest of the wiki, and of the page only its frontmatter and its body up to the end of the Now (`okfadapter.ReadHead`), so a thread page whose Timeline grew for years still shows its Now, and a brief or a prompt's refresh reads at most 64 KiB past the Now's end however long the page is. A Now that ends past 2 MiB, as one a person moved below a long section would, shows none, and a rules page over 2 MiB is not read. Markers after the Now's end are never looked at, so a page damaged only there still shows its Now. Mark it stale if `as_of` is older than the previous chat's last prompt, reply, tool or plan entry. Events, such as the session_start written just before the brief, don't count. When the project's own folder has no now.md, the brief reads the other project folders' now.md files (at most 256) to find the one whose remote matches, as after a slug collision. The folder found, its own or another, is recorded in the briefed marker for the refresh.
4. **Read live git** (800 ms limit, and never so long that the brief misses the hook's deadline; otherwise "unknown"):
   - branch, and ahead/behind from local refs
   - whether HEAD moved since the page's `head` (the count and the last 3 subjects)
   - up to 15 changed paths
   - any rebase, merge, cherry-pick or bisect in progress, or "unknown" when git can't tell
   - stash count, which git counts itself (`git rev-list --walk-reflogs --count refs/stash --`), so the 64 KiB a live git call keeps never cuts it

   Live git runs with lazy fetches off, as the code tie's calls do (`GIT_NO_LAZY_FETCH=1`, and `--no-lazy-fetch` in a clone that may be partial), so the hook never fetches; it checks no signature (`log.showSignature=false`), and a call killed at its deadline takes the processes it started with it.
5. **Assemble at most 8,000 characters on Claude and 6,000 on Codex** in this order. The footer and the newest exchange are reserved first, so the cap never cuts them: the newest message keeps its share and the message before it, usually the question it answers, at least 300 characters. Then the header, the opening request and live git; then the Now, up to its budget; then the tool activity, the plan, the earlier chats and the other branches share what is left, in that order of priority, each whole or left out; older messages take what remains. Every part is measured as the fence will show it, after cleaning and escaping, and should cleaning the whole or a longer fence still add a little, older messages and then the free text give way.
   1. header. When the previous chat, on this branch, has a backlog at brief time that a pass could take (its watermark is 600 bytes or more behind its session file, since a group needs 600 characters, or any bytes behind when the last pass stopped inside a group, whose rest needs only one more entry; and no update has found that rest too small to process yet), with updates on and a wiki set up, the line "An update for the previous chat is pending; its Now arrives with a later prompt." follows it, and the hash-triggered refresh then delivers that Now. A compact brief and a lineage brief never show it.
   2. opening request: the previous chat's first prompt on this branch with its ref (200), from a read of the first 64 KiB of its session file, never a whole file. It is left out when an off_record event comes before it there, or when an exchange below shows it.
   3. live git
   4. Now, by priority (see "The Now in a brief" under Recall and fencing): 3,200 characters on Claude and 2,400 on Codex, or 1,714 and 1,285 if stale
   5. open plan, if newer than the page (600)
   6. tool activity, a heuristic: the last test or build command with its exit code (150), meaning a shell command that starts with go test, go build, go vet, npm test, pnpm test, yarn test, pytest, make, cargo test or swift test, after any `cd <folder> &&` or `cd <folder>;` (the folder a word or quoted), shown as it ran, cd included; and up to 5 distinct files from edit and write tool lines, newest first, shown relative to the worktree
   7. earlier chats on this branch: up to 3 other chats that did real work, newest first, in about 80 characters each: the day of its newest work, the host, and its first prompt with its ref, from the same bounded head read
   8. other branches: up to 3 with real work in the project's 64 most recent session files, newest first, with the day of each one's newest work (about 60 characters each)
   9. last exchanges verbatim, newest last: 3,085 on Claude and 2,314 on Codex, or 4,571 and 3,428 if stale or there is no page. The newest message keeps half of that, and the one before it at least 300, whatever else the brief holds. Long messages are cut in the middle with an `eva-md show` ref.
   10. footer, at most 450: the installed binary's absolute path (`~/.local/share/eva-md/bin/eva-md`, since a chat's PATH may not reach eva-md), named once, with a short fetch protocol: `<bin> search <terms>` when the answer depends on earlier chats, rephrasing with other words, file names or dates on a miss and stopping after about 5 lookups; `<bin> show <ref>`, or `<thread id>#Now` or `#Timeline --last 10` when a thread page exists; check what may have changed against the code. With a wiki configured, a plain `grep -ri <terms>` fallback names the project's threads folder. With the usual binary path the footer is about 250 characters without a wiki, and about 380 with the thread id and the threads folder; a 40-character branch name brings it to about 425. Past 450 the grep fallback is left out first, then the thread id with its Now and Timeline pointers; the two commands stay.

   Three more parts are reserved with the footer, so the cap never cuts them: the standing rules, first in the brief (see "Rules page"), the project's constraints, right after them (see "Constraints" under Recall and fencing), and the system pointers, just before the footer (see "System pointers" under Recall and fencing).

   A new chat with no chat to resume and no Now gets the footer alone, about 150 characters and unfenced, pointing at `<bin> search --all` for the other projects. With standing rules, constraints or a system map it carries those too, fenced. It is not counted as history found.
6. Fence the brief and return it as `additionalContext`. Claude also gets a one-line `systemMessage`, "eva.md: earlier work is available for this chat.", when history was found, unless the config sets `notice = "problems"`; a health notice takes its place when there is one. Both hosts get a one-line notice only when the quick health checks find a problem. When the config exists but can't be read, a chat start on either host gets one line instead, "eva.md: the config can't be read, so this chat isn't captured; run eva-md doctor.", as `additionalContext` and as the `systemMessage`, and nothing is captured; every other event is as before, Codex Stop still printing `{}`. A config read cut off by the hook's time limit, a hook in eva-md's own run (`EVA_MD_INNER=1`) and a missing config give no such line. Write the per-session briefed marker last, and only with 20 ms to spare before the deadline, so a brief that was not delivered is retried by the first-prompt fallback. It records the hash of the Now section the chat has seen, the brief variant, how many refreshes have been sent, and the project's wiki folder the brief found.
7. Spawn the catch-up update (see "Triggers"), in one `eva-md update` job that catches up at most 5 sessions: first this chat's own, unless a program runs the chat, when its session file, as one stat found it before the hook appended anything, had not changed for 2 minutes or more and has a backlog, as for a chat resumed, or started again with the same id, after it was left idle; then, from the session files the brief listed in step 2 (the project's 64 most recently changed, and those of chats a program ran that it read beyond the 64; never a full inbox scan), the other sessions that have a backlog and whose file has not changed for 2 minutes or more, or that start-of-chat transcript recovery appended to in step 1 (it takes only sessions idle that long), newest first. A person's chat start also asks that job for the day's sweep, at most 5 more sessions from the whole inbox, or starts it alone when no session needs catching up, until a job has taken the sweep that day (see "The day's sweep" under "Triggers"). When a push is pending, the start also spawns `eva-md maintain --push-only`, at most every 30 minutes (see "Daily commit and push").

Host differences:
- Claude caps additionalContext at 10,000 characters, so 8,000 is a safe hard cap.
- Codex limits hook context by tokens (2,500 by default), so the Codex hooks set `additionalContextLimit` to 3000, and the brief's 6,000 characters stay well inside it.
- Codex shows systemMessage as a warning, so a healthy start is silent there.

### Resume: resumed or forked chat

SessionStart(resume|fork): the conversation is already loaded. Inject only what changed since this session's last entry:
- the standing rules, first, when a rule changed
- the Now, by priority in 700 characters, when it changed
- other sessions' activity on this thread, one line each: `Other chat: <host> at <time> (<its last activity>)` only when another chat on the branch did work (a prompt, reply, tool or plan) after this chat's last work, with that work's time; a later event alone, such as the other chat closing, adds no line
- live git

At most 1,500 characters, and nothing if nothing changed. Same on both hosts.

The catch-up update of step 7 above runs at this start too, with the same rules: 2 minutes idle, at most 5 sessions, from the session files the brief listed, and on a person's chat start the day's sweep in the same job. A chat resumed after it was left idle has its own backlog in that job, first: the start appends to its file, so its next prompt finds the file just changed and the prompt trigger would never take that backlog.

### Resume: first-prompt fallback

If no brief was delivered at SessionStart, the first UserPromptSubmit returns the full startup brief. This covers a desktop spare session, a hook trusted mid-session, or a folder that wasn't trusted yet. It is keyed by the per-session briefed marker, so it happens once.

### Resume: hash-triggered refresh

On every UserPromptSubmit after a brief, read the thread page by its path, under the wiki folder the briefed marker records (or the project's own when it records none), and compare its Now hash and the standing rules' hash with the ones in the marker. A refresh never lists or reads the other projects' folders, so it costs about what capture does. The hash is taken after the hidden comments are removed, so a change to item ids or dates alone is no change. If either changed, an update finished while you were working: inject the changed rules (see "Rules page") and, when it changed, the new Now, by priority, in at most 2,500 characters, fenced, and record the hashes and one more refresh in the marker. When neither changed, nothing is injected, no refresh is used and the marker stays as it is.

A chat gets at most 2 refreshes. A compact brief keeps the count, so compacting a chat never grants more; any other brief, such as after a /clear, starts it again. A Now saved after a compaction still reaches the chat while it has a refresh left. A marker written before the count, with `"refreshed": true`, counts as one refresh used. As with the brief, the marker is saved only with 20 ms to spare before the deadline, so a refresh that was not delivered is retried on the next prompt.

### After compaction

1. PreCompact: catch up, append a compact event and spawn an update for the session. Exit within 1 s with no output.
2. The host compacts the conversation.
3. Claude PostCompact: store `compact_summary`, unless the chat has been off the record since its last startup or clear.
4. SessionStart(compact): inject the header, this session's opening request (from the same bounded head read), live git, the thread's Now by priority (2,100 characters on Claude, 1,575 on Codex), the open plan, the tool activity line, this session's last 2 exchanges before compaction, verbatim (2,250 and 1,687), and the footer. At most 6,000 characters on Claude and 4,500 on Codex in total, with the footer and the newest exchange reserved first, and the tool activity line before the Now takes its budget. If the update has not finished, the brief says the Now section predates these exchanges. The catch-up update of a chat start (step 7 of "Resume: new chat or /clear") runs here too, with the same rules; the chat's own file changed at PreCompact, which spawned its update, so it is left out unless the compaction took 2 minutes or more.
5. The hash-triggered refresh applies here too: when the update for the compacted part finishes, the next prompt carries the new Now, if the chat has a refresh left (the count runs on through compactions).

Codex runs SessionStart(compact) before the next model request, even after automatic compaction mid-turn.

### Background update

The job, `eva-md update` and the triggers that spawn it are built and tested offline, with fake host CLIs and a stand-in for the spawned binary; no live update has run yet.

1. A trigger (see "Triggers") spawns the installed binary, `~/.local/share/eva-md/bin/eva-md`, as `eva-md update --session <host>:<id>`, with one `--session` for each session the start-of-chat catch-up names, once the hook's output is written. The child runs in a session of its own (setsid), out of reach of the signals a host sends the hook, with stdin from /dev/null, stdout and stderr appended to `<state>/logs/update-<YYYY-MM-DD>.log` (0600), the update's allowlisted environment with `EVA_MD_INNER=1` (see "Model selection"), and `/` as its folder, so it holds none of the hook's pipes. The hook starts it and releases it, never waiting; a spawn that fails is ignored, and the next trigger tries again. `eva-md update --pending` drains every session with a backlog, and `--foreground` runs attached, as a person does.
2. With `updates = false` in the config, stop at once (`paused`). Take the one global lock, `<locks>/update.lock`, without waiting; if another job holds it, exit 0, and the next trigger tries again. The hooks' session lock is never taken. Set aside, once, the chats captured before updates (see "Job control"). Check the wiki's schema version and instance against the config.
3. Drain the session's backlog oldest first, unless the budget, a hold or a backoff of its host, or a quarantine of the session today, already applies: then its file is not opened (see "Job control"). A program-run chat (see "A program's chat" under "Job control") is passed over as `skipped (headless)`: the watermark moves past the window with no model call. A session whose project the config now excludes is left as it is, with no call and its watermark where it was. Decode a bounded window past the watermark, take the next (session, branch) group and let `Build` cut its oldest chunk, at most 60 KB. A group under one exchange or 600 characters waits for more entries, and the watermark records the file's size then, so the triggers and the brief leave the session alone until something is appended.
4. Read the thread page by its path. A locked, human-verified or damaged page is skipped with its reason, and the watermark moves on. Otherwise check the daily cap of model calls, a hold or backoff of the host and the session's quarantine, save the watermark as it stands (one that can't be saved stops the pass before the call), resolve the effort, and run the host CLI headless in an empty run folder. The model is chosen when the pass is planned. The timeout scales with effort, from 180 s to 600 s.
5. Validate.
6. Save the knowledge pages the pass kept changes for (see "Knowledge page"), then the rules pages, then the thread page. Apply the update to the page, render it and save it through the adapter's compare-and-swap; on a conflict, read the page again and apply again, at most twice. A page whose rendered body equals the body read back keeps its body, and only its `as_of` and `sources` are stamped, when the pass moves them. After a save, regenerate the project's now.md and the index files of the folders the write touched.
7. Move the watermark to exactly the end of the chunk, after the page's folder fsync when it changed, and append the pass to the day's run log. Then take the next chunk.

Details are in the next section.

### Daily commit and push

Built in `internal/wikigit` and run by `eva-md maintain`, or by hand with `eva-md commit`.

1. **Trigger.** With a wiki path and an instance in the config, or with no wiki at all, for maintain's search index step, the first hook event of the local day that passes the guards creates `<state>/commit/attempt-<date>` with O_EXCL once its output is written, and spawns `eva-md maintain` detached like the update (see "Background update"), with SSH_AUTH_SOCK, GNUPGHOME, GIT_SSH, GIT_SSH_COMMAND, GIT_CONFIG_GLOBAL, GIT_CONFIG_NOSYSTEM and XDG_CONFIG_HOME kept as well, so git can sign and push. A capture that misses its deadline spawns nothing and leaves the day unused. A spawn that fails is ignored, and the day's commit waits for the next day or a manual run.
2. **maintain** takes `<locks>/maintain.lock` without waiting (a busy run exits 0), builds the files that list the knowledge pages again when they are out of date (see "Knowledge step" below), runs the commit and push unless `commits = false`, then the lint (`internal/lint`, which records thread pages over 512 KB and refs the inbox no longer resolves in `<state>/lint.json`), then takes the code status of the system pages, as `eva-md system status` does (a wiki without system pages has none to take, and only one that can't be read fails the step), then removes attempt markers older than 14 days. It prints at most one line a step, counts and reasons only; the knowledge step prints one only when it rebuilt something or found the wiki busy.
3. Take the commit lock without waiting (a busy run is recorded as `busy`), then the wiki lock, waiting up to 60 s for running writes.
4. **Guards**, in this order, each a refusal with its reason: no wiki; an origin or push URL other than the configured remote (`origin`); an `instance` in `.eva-md/schema.toml` other than the config's; a `schema_version` other than 1, as a newer eva-md writes (`schema`); a `.git/index.lock` (`index_lock`, recorded with its age and never deleted); a rebase, merge, cherry-pick or revert in progress (`rebase`, `merge`, `cherry_pick`, `revert`); unmerged paths in the index, which `git ls-files --unmerged` lists, as a conflicted `git stash pop`, `git checkout -m` or `git apply --3way` leaves them with no state file (`conflicts`), since `git add` would mark them resolved, conflict markers and all; a detached HEAD (`detached`).
5. `git add` the explicit paths that exist: projects, general, systems, knowledge, index.md, log.md, README.md and .eva-md, which holds the names index, the repos index and the recall index. A path gone from disk that git still tracks (`git ls-files` lists it), such as a knowledge folder removed whole, is added too, so its removal is committed; one git doesn't know is left out, as `git add` would fail on it.
6. Read the staged names and leave out, recording each path in the run's `unstaged` list: paths outside those, symlinks and submodules, `.jsonl` files, inbox paths, `.tmp-okf-*` and `.tmp-eva-*` files, and the secret hits of step 8. A left-out path still staged when the index is read again fails the run (`unstage`).
7. When anything is left to scan, run okf Validate (Drift on, Strict off) and record its error count. Errors never stop the commit: saving memory matters more than a clean report.
8. **Secret scan** of the staged diff, line by line (see the Redactor row): a file with a flagged added line, a binary file or an added line over 1 MiB is left out. Nothing is redacted in place. git runs without the variables that change how it reads a pathspec (`GIT_LITERAL_PATHSPECS`, `GIT_GLOB_PATHSPECS`, `GIT_NOGLOB_PATHSPECS` and `GIT_ICASE_PATHSPECS`), as it runs without those that would point it at another repository, so the literal paths steps 5, 6 and 8 name mean what they say whatever your shell exports.
9. Release the wiki lock. With nothing staged, the run's outcome is `nothing`, and it pushes only when HEAD is ahead of its upstream.
10. `git commit -m "eva-md daily 2026-01-15: 12 pages"` ("1 page" for one; knowledge pages and the knowledge map count, index files and the root log.md don't) with your signing as configured, and a 300 s timeout, since an approval can take a while. A failed commit is `failed` with a reason that git's diagnostics sort, without keeping their text: `commit_signer_missing` when git can't start the signing program, as one a desktop app's short PATH doesn't reach; `commit_signature` when the signature failed or was declined; `commit_timeout`; or `commit` for any other failure. It is not retried until the next day or a manual `eva-md commit`.
11. `git push --porcelain origin HEAD` right after, with a 60 s timeout, never forced. The result is `ok`, `up_to_date`, `diverged` for a non-fast-forward, which waits for a person, `failed`, or `pending` when the host is out of reach, the push timed out or wasn't approved, or a local remote is missing.
12. Append the run to `<state>/commits/<YYYY-MM>.jsonl` (0600): `at`, `outcome` (committed, nothing, refused, failed or busy), `reason`, `pages`, `validate_errors`, `unstaged`, `push` (ok, up_to_date, pending, diverged, failed or skipped) and `lock_age_s`. Counts, paths and reasons only.

**Push retry.** At a chat start with commits on, unless this hook just started the day's maintain, the hook checks for a pending push at most every 30 minutes: between checks it costs one stat of `<state>/commit/push-retry`; a check reads up to 16 KB of each of the last two months' commit records and sets that marker. The newest record that is neither busy nor a run with nothing to commit that skipped its push decides: only `pending` is retried, by spawning `eva-md maintain --push-only`, which pushes only while that is still so. Refused runs, failed commits and `diverged`, `failed`, `ok` or `up_to_date` pushes are never retried. A branch without an upstream, such as a wiki set up with `--no-push`, never pushes on a day with nothing to commit; only this retry pushes it.

`commits = false` keeps the commit, the push and the retry out of maintain, which still runs the lint, the code status and the search index; `eva-md commit` by hand ignores it. The lines page saves add to log.md go out with the day's commit.

**Knowledge step.** First, unless `--push-only`, when `knowledge/` exists, or a recall index or root index line left from it does (`knowledgepage.Report.Exists`), and eva-md may write the wiki, its schema being schema_version 1 and naming the config's instance (`health.WritableWiki`, the doctor's gate), maintain compares the knowledge map, `knowledge/index.md`, the recall index and the root index's line with what the pages give, and builds those out of date again under the wiki lock (`knowledgepage.Refresh`), so the same day's commit holds them. It prints `maintain: knowledge: rebuilt the index (N pages)`, N counting every knowledge page, only when it wrote something, and nothing otherwise. A wiki lock that stays busy prints `maintain: knowledge: the wiki stayed busy; the next run builds the index again` and fails nothing; any other error goes to stderr as `eva-md: knowledge: <error>` and makes maintain exit 1.

**Recovery records.** After the attempt markers, maintain removes the transcript-recovery records, `<state>/catchup/<host>/<session>.json`, not saved for 30 days, and those past the newest 2,048 of their host, newest by when each was last saved, so the chat-start catch-up, which lists at most 4,096 a host, still finds the newest; it never removes the drift records beside those folders. The hooks save a record only under its session's lock, so maintain removes one only while it holds that lock, taken without waiting and by the session the record names, and only when the record is still the one it listed, which it checks once it holds that lock: a record whose session a hook holds, or that a hook saved again meanwhile, even after maintain read it, stays for the next run. A record it can't read, which no hook saves again, goes as listed. A chat whose record is gone starts again at its transcript's end at its next event, as a new chat does: nothing is read twice, rows its hooks missed before then are never recovered, and whether it is off the record still comes from the session's marker, so the record of a chat off the record stays while that marker isn't set, as when it couldn't be written, until recovery writes it at the chat's next pass. It prints `maintain: transcript recovery: removed N records` only when it removed some; a record it can't remove waits for the next run. The hooks never prune.

**Search index.** Maintain brings the search index up to date (see "Search index"), last when a wiki is set up, and prints `maintain: index: ...`, such as `2 files (3.1 MB) indexed in 1 project, 12.0 MB in all` or `up to date (12.0 MB)`, or `busy`; a failure, such as a disk too full to write a segment, exits 1. Without a wiki, maintain runs only this step, then the pruning of attempt markers and recovery records, so a capture-only install gets a daily index too; the hooks start it there once a day, but never a push retry, and a wiki path without the instance init sets still starts nothing. `--push-only` without a wiki is refused, as before.

Plain git only. A wiki on a self-hosted server that is out of reach just leaves its push pending.

### Setup and init of a new wiki

`eva-md setup` is the first-run experience, run in a terminal. It starts with a checklist, before any question, so a person fixes everything first and runs setup again:
- **Checking this Mac.** A block headed "Checking this Mac" runs the checks in order and stops at the first that fails: macOS (`runtime.GOOS`); git on PATH; the host apps `--host` or `EVA_MD_HOSTS` asks for (the `claude` and `codex` commands; for `all`, at least one, and only those found get the plugins); the answers file, when present, read without errors (a bad value, a remote setup can't use, a link, a folder, a file over 16 KB or not UTF-8); and on a Mac not set up yet, when the wiki's host is known, the CLI for it (below). Each check that passes prints "✓ <what was found>"; the first that fails prints "✗ <what is wrong>", then an indented "Fix: <the exact command or step>" and "Then run setup again.", and setup exits 1 having asked and changed nothing after it. The marks are coloured (green ✓, red ✗, yellow !) only when stdout is a terminal and `NO_COLOR` is unset or empty; otherwise the text is the same without escape codes. Lines name only the paths and host names a person typed to name their folder, repo and host, never another value from the answers file. Commit signing is information, read from the home folder as git reads a boolean (`git config --get --type=bool commit.gpgsign`, so true, yes, on, 1 and a bare key are on), with `gpg.format` naming the kind, `openpgp` when unset: "✓ Commit signing on (<kind>)", or "! Commit signing off: …", which never stops setup. Last come install's own checks, on every run: install as a preflight (`install.Options.Preflight`), a dry run that needs no config yet, with this binary and the hosts setup will install for, within the 2 minutes an install has, so that anything install would refuse before it changes anything stops setup there, before it asks anything or makes or pushes a wiki: Codex's hooks feature off, a host command that fails, another eva-md registration, a Codex marketplace registered without its folder, a registration that needs attention, or eva-md's folders holding files it didn't make. It passes as "✓ The plugins can be installed for <apps>". Codex's hooks feature off is "✗ Codex's hooks feature is off, so eva-md can't capture Codex chats", with "Fix: run codex features enable hooks, or set EVA_MD_HOSTS=claude in <answers file> to install for Claude Code only", or "run setup with --host claude" when `--host` chose the hosts; on a Mac with no `claude` command, which setup looks for whatever `--host` chose, the second way starts "install Claude Code, then". A host command that fails says to run that command, whose own words install never shows; any other refusal is "✗ The plugins can't be installed: <what install says>", with install's fix, or for a conflicting registration, to remove it with that app's plugin commands, and otherwise "fix that first; setup makes no wiki until install's checks pass". A broken `config.toml`, and flags setup can't parse, stop it before the block, as before.
- **The wiki host's CLI.** The host is the remote's, from `--remote` or `EVA_MD_WIKI_REMOTE`, or else the git host, from `--git-host` or `EVA_MD_GIT_HOST_URL`; a local remote has none. A remote on GitHub's or GitLab's SSH host, ssh.github.com or altssh.gitlab.com, on any port, is read as on github.com or gitlab.com before the name rule (below); a git host is used as it is named. An alias from `~/.ssh/config` is never resolved: ssh reads that file from the account's own home folder whatever `HOME` says, so setup neither reads it nor runs `ssh -G`. The CLIs are asked as "GitHub or GitLab" (below) says. One signed in there passes ("✓ glab signed in to gitlab.example.com"); both pass in a terminal, where setup asks later which it is, and fail without one ("Fix: run setup in a terminal to choose"). With none ready and only the git host, setup would have to create the repo and can't, so the check fails with the fix for the CLI the host's name says: "✗ glab isn't installed" with "Fix: install glab (brew install glab), then run glab auth login --hostname <host>", "✗ glab isn't signed in to <host>" with "Fix: glab auth login --hostname <host>", or one that didn't answer in time; for a name that says neither, a plain git server, "✗ Setup can't create the repo on <host>: no gh or glab is signed in there" with "Fix: create the repo yourself and give its address in EVA_MD_WIKI_REMOTE or --remote". With none ready and a remote given, the CLI is no prerequisite: a "!" line says what isn't ready and that "setup will check the repo is private by reading it without signing in", such as "! gh isn't signed in to github.com: …" or "! No gh or glab is signed in to <host>: …", and setup goes on with the read with no sign-in below, as it did before it had checks. With no host known yet, that is, with no remote and no git host given, as in a terminal with no answers, the block lists as information which of gh (at github.com) and glab (at its default host) are installed and signed in where, saying "glab can't name its default host" when `glab config get host` fails or names one setup can't use, and setup goes on; what they said is kept, so the remote question asks neither again. A Mac already set up skips this check and these lines: there gh or glab only reads, when it can, the address to clone the wiki again from.

Then it looks before it asks, and asks only what it can't work out:
- **A Mac already set up** keeps its wiki. A healthy one prints "eva-md is set up here: …" and goes straight to the plugins question, with no summary. A missing or empty wiki folder (one holding only Finder's `.DS_Store` counts as empty) has its repo read, as a chosen repo is, before the question below, or after a yes to it when no is its default: an empty repo, or one with another eva wiki, stops setup, since cloned again either would become a new wiki in place of the configured one; init refuses that too. Otherwise it gets "Clone it again from <address> into <dir>? [Y/n]", defaulting to no when the folder's parent is missing too; that yes is the only confirmation, the clone is never pushed, and the address is `--remote`, a local remote's path, the address gh or glab gives for the same repo, or else `git@<host>:<path>.git`. Any other mismatch (not a clone, another origin, no schema, another instance, a file in the folder's place, a folder that can't be read) stops with one sentence ending "restore your wiki there, or move <config> aside to start over". A `--dir` or `--remote` that would change the wiki is refused.
- **A new Mac** gets the two choices that have no default: the folder for the wiki, and the remote it is pushed to. A folder that already holds an eva wiki is offered for use, unless it has a commit and uncommitted changes, which init would refuse (setup first removes the lock file a save killed midway leaves in the wiki's root, which is never yours, and says so instead when it can't, or when the wiki stays busy); a wiki whose first commit failed, as when the signature was declined, has its scaffold staged and no HEAD, so it is offered ("Use it? [Y/n]"), and init then checks the scaffold, stages it and makes the first commit; a repository with commits that isn't an eva wiki, a clone or not, and one with no commits and no remote, are refused, and so is an eva wiki with no remote (add it back with `git remote add origin <address>`), with a remote setup can't use (point it at the wiki's repo with `git remote set-url origin <address>`), with a local remote path that isn't there now, as on a volume that isn't mounted (mount or restore it), or whose schema names no instance; a `file://` remote is one setup can't use. A folder holding only Finder's `.DS_Store` counts as empty. A chosen repo is read before the summary, from a shallow copy in a temporary folder (`git clone --no-local --depth 1 --no-checkout`, with only `.eva-md/schema.toml` checked out, removed afterwards), so the wiki folder is never touched: an empty repo gets a new wiki, an eva wiki made on this Mac is used, one made on another Mac, by its machine label compared without the domain, asks "Use it? [y/N]", and other content or a wiki of a schema this eva-md can't use is refused. A git that runs out of time reading the repo fails the read, saying so, so a repo that is slow to read is never taken for an empty one or for one with other content.
- **The answers file.** `config.env` in eva-md's config folder (`~/.config/eva-md/config.env`, beside `config.toml`, which stays eva-md's own settings file that setup writes) holds answers a person fills in for setup. It is read strictly: a regular file, never through a link (`O_NOFOLLOW`, `O_NONBLOCK`), at most 16 KB, UTF-8. Lines are `KEY=value`; blank lines and lines starting with `#` are skipped; a leading `export ` is allowed; the value is everything after the first `=`, trimmed, with one pair of matching double or single quotes removed, and taken literally, with no escapes and no variable or command expansion. A key given twice keeps its last value. The keys are `EVA_MD_WIKI_DIR` (as `--dir`: a full path or `~/...`), `EVA_MD_WIKI_REMOTE` (as `--remote`), `EVA_MD_GIT_HOST_URL` (as `--git-host`, which takes the same values: a web address, `https://` or `http://`, with any path, query or fragment, such as a profile or projects page, or a bare host name with or without a path; setup uses only the host, in lower case, keeping a port and dropping a trailing dot, with www.github.com and www.gitlab.com read as github.com and gitlab.com; a user name or password in the address, another scheme or a repo's address, `ssh://` or `git@host:path`, whose message says a repo's address goes in `EVA_MD_WIKI_REMOTE`, and an empty or malformed host are refused, naming the key or the flag, never the value), `EVA_MD_HOSTS` (as `--host`), `EVA_MD_ALLOW_INTERNAL_REPO` (`true` or `false`, any case, the answer in advance to the internal question; see "Internal repos" below; written empty in the template and in the file setup fills in) and `EVA_MD_CODEX_IGNORE_USER_CONFIG` (`true` or `false`, any case, sets `codex_ignore_user_config` in `config.toml` after the plugins are installed, saying so when it changes it, and refusing to rewrite a config that holds keys eva-md doesn't know). An empty value is an answer not given, and a flag wins over the file. A key setup doesn't know is named, never its value, in one line on stderr and otherwise ignored; only a key shaped like setup's own (`EVA_MD_` then capitals, digits and underscores) is named, and any other line by its number, since it may hold a secret pasted by mistake, such as a base64 token whose `=` padding makes it look like `KEY=`; a bad value (an unknown host name, a switch that isn't true or false, a git host address it refuses, a relative folder, a remote setup can't use, or a line that isn't `KEY=value`, named by its number) stops setup with a message naming the key or line and the file. A remote that is a local path that isn't there now gets its own message, "<key> in <file> names <path>, which isn't there now; mount or restore it, or give an SSH or HTTPS address"; on a Mac already set up such a path is no error, and is matched with the configured remote by its path, as a missing local origin is, so a volume that isn't mounted never stops setup there. On a new Mac, the file's folder and repo are setup's answers: neither question is asked, the summary adds "Answers from <file>" and the confirmation is asked as before; `--yes` and a run without a terminal treat them as `--dir` and `--remote`. When the file is missing on a new Mac, setup writes the template (0600, `O_EXCL`, so it never replaces anything, a link included) once the checks before it pass, and says so in the checks' next line, "✓ Wrote <file>, a template for setup's answers", so a later check that stops setup never leaves it unsaid; in a terminal with a question to ask it adds "Fill in <file> and run setup again, or answer here." and asks as before; without a terminal and without `--dir` and `--remote` it stops (exit 2) saying it wrote the template. On a Mac already set up, a missing file is written after a successful setup (once the plugins are installed), filled in with what this Mac now uses: the wiki folder as the config has it, under `~/` when it is in the home folder; the address its clone's origin is pushed to, or the remote setup used, when it names the configured repo, but never a local remote, left empty so that a volume that isn't mounted never stops a later run; the git host that address names, as `EVA_MD_GIT_HOST_URL=https://<host>`; the hosts as `all`, the template's value, since a flag such as `--host` is for its run only; and the Codex switch as `config.toml` has it, `true` or `false`. It is written as the template is, 0600 and never over anything, in the template's words with a header saying setup filled it in, a value quoted when reading it back would change it and one with a line break refused, and setup says so in one line. On a Mac already set up, a folder or repo in the file must name the configured wiki and remote, as the flags must, and a mismatch stops setup naming the file and the key.
- **GitHub or GitLab.** The host's name says first, ignoring a port and letter case: github.com, a name holding `github` (as a label or part of one) or one ending in `.ghe.com` is GitHub, worked with through gh pointed at that host; gitlab.com or a name holding `gitlab`, such as gitlab.example.com or code-gitlab.example.org, is GitLab, through glab pointed at that host. When the name tells, setup asks that CLI first, and the other only when that one isn't ready there; one signed in there is used, so a GitHub Enterprise server whose name holds gitlab, or a GitLab whose name holds github, works with the CLI signed in to it. When the named CLI is ready, the other is never run, and when neither is, the check's line names the CLI the name says (above). A name holding both, or neither, is unclear: setup asks `gh auth status --hostname <host>` and `glab auth status --hostname <host>` at once, each within its time limit. When exactly one is signed in, setup uses it; when neither is, the host is a plain git server; when both are, it asks "Is it GitHub or GitLab? [gh/glab]" in a terminal, after a line saying both are signed in and with "Type gh or glab" after an answer it doesn't understand, three of which stop setup; without a terminal it stops saying it can't tell. Every call about the repo points the CLI at the host, `GH_HOST=<host>` for gh and `GITLAB_HOST=<host>` for glab, in that CLI's environment for that call only, never in eva-md's own; glab is asked about a known host directly, `glab auth status --hostname <host>` with `GITLAB_HOST=<host>`, and never for its default host then; `glab config get host`, which gets no host from setup, is asked only for the information lines when no host is known. No token from the environment reaches either CLI, on any call and whatever the host: `GH_TOKEN`, `GITHUB_TOKEN`, `GH_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN`, `GITHUB_ENTERPRISE_TOKEN`, `GITLAB_TOKEN`, `GITLAB_ACCESS_TOKEN`, `OAUTH_TOKEN` and `CI_JOB_TOKEN` are left out of its environment, so each uses only its own sign-in (`gh auth login`, `glab auth login`), which `auth status --hostname <host>` checks, and a token meant for one host is never sent to another that setup points the CLI at. This choice is made wherever setup picks a CLI: to check a typed or `--remote` repo, to read a repo's address to clone a missing wiki again (where the question, its explanation and its hint go to setup's output, and running out of answers stops setup with nothing cloned), and to offer to create the repo.
- **Creating the repo.** With a git host given (`--git-host` or `EVA_MD_GIT_HOST_URL`), setup offers to create the repo only there, with the CLI chosen as above, which the checks have found ready. The repo goes under the account that CLI is signed in as, named `eva-wiki` or what the user types, such as `group/eva-wiki`; the path of the git host's address is never used. Without one, when gh (github.com) or glab (its default host) is installed and signed in, setup offers to create a private repo there, `eva-wiki` or a typed name; with both, "Which one? [GH/glab/n]", where Enter picks gh. An existing repo of that name is used when it is private, or internal after the internal question below, at once when empty and after "use it as your wiki? [Y/n]" and the read above when not; a public one is refused, and so is an internal one without a yes. Setup reads back the SSH address, or HTTPS when that CLI's git protocol is https, and then says how git can sign in. Any failure, a timeout included, falls back to the address prompt with one line saying why, or, for a refusal setup recognises (see "Refusals" below), to the name question again when another name or a fix can work. A create that timed out counts as possibly made, and when setup stops after making a repo it says the repo stays for the next setup. glab's default host is the one `glab config get host` names, from `GITLAB_HOST` or glab's own config, or gitlab.com when neither sets one; for a self-hosted GitLab, name it as the git host instead. When HTTPS is gh's protocol on a host other than github.com, the sign-in hint is `gh auth setup-git --hostname <host>`.
- **A typed or `--remote` address** is refused when the CLI for its host, chosen as above, says the repo is public, and asked about when it says internal (see "Internal repos" below); a remote on ssh.github.com or altssh.gitlab.com is asked about on github.com or gitlab.com. When no CLI is signed in to the host, git tries to read the repo over HTTPS with nothing that could sign it in, within 10 s: none of your git config, so no credential helper, header or URL rewrite; an empty home folder, so no `.netrc`; no Kerberos ticket and no empty-user authentication that would offer one; no proxy and no prompt. Setup says first which repo it checks ("Checking whether <host/path> can be read without signing in."). A repo it reads may be public: with a terminal setup asks "Use it anyway? [y/N]", since a host can let its own network read private repos too, and without one it refuses it. An internal repo there, and local paths, are not checked.
- **Refusals.** Setup never prints, logs or keeps the messages gh, glab or git print when they refuse or fail, which can show account details; what it prints from them is only the repo address `repo view` gives for a repo it found or created. `repo create`'s stdout and stderr go to one buffer that keeps their first 64 KiB, never fails a write and is read only to sort a failure into a reason, by case-insensitive substrings of known English wordings, with every run of white space read as one space (glab v1.127.0 prints errors through fang v2.0.1, which word-wraps them at 116 columns, indents each line and pads it with spaces, so a line break can fall inside a wording), each quoted from its source in a comment (`cmd/eva-md/setup_refusals.go`); the error that travels up carries only the reason. The create reasons: private (or internal) visibility restricted by the host's admins (GitLab's "<level> has been restricted by your GitLab administrator"); gh's "internal repositories can only be created within an organization"; the name taken (GitHub's "name already exists on this account", GitLab's "has already been taken"); a project limit (GitLab's "You cannot create projects in your personal namespace" and "You've reached your limit of"); no permission in that group or organization (GitLab's namespace "is not valid", glab's "could not find group or namespace", GitHub's "You need admin access to the organization" and "does not have the correct permissions to execute"); the sign-in refused (GitHub's "Bad credentials", GitLab's "401 Unauthorized" and its `invalid_token` error for an expired or revoked token); and the host unreachable (gh's "check your internet connection", which it prints for a name that doesn't resolve, Go's "no such host", "connection refused", "network is unreachable", "no route to host", "operation timed out", "i/o timeout", "x509: " and "tls: "). Each prints "<cli> couldn't create <name>: <reason in setup's words>." and a next step: for private restricted, the internal question (below) and, without a yes, that neither the CLI nor you can make a private repo there, so ask an admin there to create an empty private repo and paste its address, keep the wiki in a local repo only your account can read (`mkdir -m 700 ~/eva-wiki.git && git init --bare ~/eva-wiki.git`, then its full path; git keeps the mode of a folder that is there, and a 0755 one in a home folder every local account can enter would let them clone it) or use another host that allows private repos; for no permission, a group or organization typed as `group/eva-wiki`, an owner of that group or organization, or `n` and the local repo; for a limit, a group or organization typed as `group/eva-wiki`, the host's admins on a host other than github.com and gitlab.com (on those it names no one), or `n` and the local repo; for a taken name, another name or `n`; for a refused sign-in, `<cli> auth login --hostname <host>` in another terminal, then the name again; for an unreachable host, the network or VPN. All but private restricted ask for the name again, three times at most; on the last try the step never says to type a name: for a taken name, a refused sign-in or an unreachable host it says to run setup again, and for no permission or a limit it keeps who to ask and the local repo. When offer then gives no repo, the address prompt asks for what the steps named (`addressHint`): once the host refused a private repo, whatever followed, and after a limit or no permission, "Paste the repo's SSH or HTTPS address, or a local repo's full path."; after a taken name, "Paste the existing repo's SSH or HTTPS address."; after a repo created now whose address was lost, the address of an empty private repository; otherwise "Create an empty private repository first" as before. Anything else prints "<cli> couldn't create <name>." as before. Reading a repo (setup's shallow clone, for `checkRemote` and `sameWiki`), wikigit's runner keeps git's stderr only to sort it: besides the classes a commit's record names, which are unchanged, it sets a read reason (`Error.Read`), which nothing records: not found (GitLab's "The project you were looking for could not be found or you don't have permission to view it", GitHub's "Repository not found", git's "does not appear to be a git repository", "repository '…' not found" and "repository '…' does not exist"), an SSH agent that declined to sign (ssherr.c's "agent refused operation", which OpenSSH prints before its "Permission denied"), an SSH key refused (OpenSSH's "Permission denied (publickey"), a changed host key (sshconnect.c's "REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED" and "has changed and you have requested strict checking") and a revoked one ("REVOKED HOST KEY DETECTED" and "was revoked and you have requested strict checking", or "revoked by file" for a key in ssh's RevokedHostKeys file, where an error checking that file gives no reason), checked before the line all three end with, the banner alone being enough, an unknown host key ("Host key verification failed"), no HTTPS sign-in (git's "terminal prompts disabled", "could not read Username", "Authentication failed"), and an unreachable host (OpenSSH's and curl's "Could not resolve host…", "Connection refused", "Connection timed out", "Operation timed out", "Network is unreachable", "No route to host", curl's "Failed to connect to"), checked last, after what the host answered, with white space read as on the create side. ssh's "Control socket connect(…)" line is left out first: with ControlMaster no, its default, and a ControlPath set, ssh prints it with "Connection refused" for a control socket whose master has exited, then connects without it, so it is about a socket on the Mac, never the host. Setup's messages name only the host of the address you gave, and the SSH hints (`ssh -T git@<host>`, with the address's user, or none when it names none, as git signs in, and its port) only for an SSH address: not found, "create it first as an empty private repo, or check the address" (when cloning a wiki again, "check the address"); a refused key, add the key to your account there, `ssh -T` shows which account it signs in as, or use the HTTPS address; an agent that didn't sign, approve its request and try again; an unknown host key, run `ssh -T` once and, if the fingerprint it shows is the host's, answer yes; a changed host key, that this can mean someone is intercepting the connection, so ask the host's admins whether the key changed and only then delete the line of known_hosts that `ssh -T` names as the offending key (ssh names it under the name and port its config gives the host, which the address may not), never to accept it; a revoked host key, that this can mean a stolen key is being used to impersonate the host, so ask the host's admins about its key (removing the host's lines from known_hosts would keep the revocation and lose the key ssh trusts); no HTTPS sign-in, set up git's sign-in for that host or use the SSH address; unreachable, check the network or VPN; a local path with no repo, "git found no repo at <path>; make one there with git init --bare, in a folder only your account can read, or give another address". A reason that doesn't fit the address, or none, gives "git couldn't read this repo; check its address and that git can sign in to it", as before. `sameWiki` puts the remote before the message.
- **Internal repos.** An internal repo is readable by everyone signed in to its GitLab, or by its GitHub enterprise. Setup still creates the repo private first. When that fails because the host's admins restrict private visibility, it asks "Create <name> as an internal repo instead? Everyone signed in to <host> can read an internal repo, and the wiki holds summaries of your chats. [y/N]" ("Every member of its enterprise on <host>" for gh), and on a yes runs the same CLI's `repo create --internal`, whose failure is sorted as above; a no, Enter or no answer creates nothing internal. An internal repo given at the question, by `--remote`, in `EVA_MD_WIKI_REMOTE`, as the folder's wiki's origin, or found under the offered name gets "This repo is internal. … Use it anyway? [y/N]" (or "<name> already exists on <host> and is internal. …"), no being the default; a no refuses it as before. `EVA_MD_ALLOW_INTERNAL_REPO` in the answers file answers in advance: `true` creates or uses one without asking, printing a "!" line for each, "! Creating <name> as an internal repo, as EVA_MD_ALLOW_INTERNAL_REPO allows: everyone signed in to <host> can read it." before it creates one, and "! This repo is internal, as …" or "! <name> is internal, as …" for one it uses ("every member of its enterprise on <host>" for gh); `true` covers every internal repo setup meets, on any host, not only one on a host that refuses private repos; `false` refuses one without asking; empty asks in a terminal and, without one, refuses it, saying to set the key to allow one. A yes typed at the question holds for that host for the rest of the run. A public repo is always refused. A Mac already set up never checks its repo's visibility (its setup only keeps the wiki or clones it again, where gh or glab is asked only for the address), so an upgrade never asks.
- **A local repo.** An absolute path to a bare repo on this Mac is a remote setup accepts (`wikigit.RemoteIdentity`), at the question, by `--remote` or in `EVA_MD_WIKI_REMOTE`. Setup's words make it with `mkdir -m 700` first, so only the user's account can read it; `~` is expanded in neither, so a path starting with it is refused as an address setup can't use. The wiki then exists only on this Mac, with a backup such as Time Machine its only copy elsewhere. Moving it to a hosted repo later is not a built-in step: setup refuses a remote other than the configured one on a Mac set up, so it is done by hand (`git remote set-url origin`, a push, and `expected_remote` in `config.toml`). Setup doesn't offer a local repo by itself; it only names it as a way on.
- The CLIs run from `/` with git's environment minus their repo, colour, debug and prompt settings, with no stdin, stderr discarded (but for `repo create`, read only to sort a failure, above) and a 10 s limit (30 s to create). No token from the environment reaches either (above). Only setup runs them (see "Changes to decisions").

It shows a summary, with one sentence of what will happen and "(created just now)" beside a repo it made, and asks "Set up eva.md with these choices? [y/N]"; every other yes/no question has a default that Enter accepts. Then it runs the init steps below, installs the plugins (next section) and runs the health check. Without a terminal setup itself reads no repo's content, creates no repo, clones no missing wiki again and asks nothing: a new Mac needs `--dir` and `--remote`, or both in the answers file, whose visibility is still checked, another Mac's wiki is adopted without a warning, and a Mac already set up stops on a mismatch or a missing or empty folder.

Before init pushes a branch the remote has never had, setup prints "The first commit is by <name> <email>, signed." (or "not signed.").
